497→def good_quality(label: str) -> bool:\n   498→    label = label.strip().lower()\n   499→    return label not in {\"garbage\", \"gabrga\", \"noisy\", \"real?\"}\n   500→\n   501→\n   502→def interp_to_taxis(old_t: np.ndarray, values: np.ndarray, new_t: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n   503→    old_t = np.asarray(old_t, dtype=np.float64).reshape(-1)\n   504→    values = np.asarray(values, dtype=np.float64).reshape(-1)\n   505→    mask = np.isfinite(old_t) & np.isfinite(values)\n   506→    if mask.sum() < 2:\n   507→        return np.full_like(new_t, np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n   508→ 0.5\n   516→    return robust_mode(bitstart) / fs - robust_mode(raw[\"events\"][\"bitStart\"])\n   517→\n   518→\n   519→def feature_xy(raw: dict, view_idx: int, feature_name: str, align_times: np.ndarray, time_vec: np.ndarray) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\n   520→    trials = raw[\"traj\"][view_idx]\n   521→    taxis = time_vec.copy()\n   522→    vidshift = find_video_offset(raw)\n   523→    xpos = np.full((time_vec.size, len(trials)), np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n   524→    ypos = np.full((time_vec.size, len(trials)), np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n   525→\n   526→    for trix, trial in enumerate(trials):\n   527→        if not math.isfinite(trial[\"n_dropped\"]):\n   528→            continue\n   529→        if feature_name not in trial[\"feat_names\"]:\n   530→            continue\n   531→        feat_idx = trial[\"feat_names\"].index(feature_name)\n   532→        ts = np.asarray(trial[\"ts\"][:, :2, feat_idx], dtype=np.float64)\n   533→        if ts.ndim != 2 or ts.shape[0] == 0:\n   534→            continue\n   535→        frame_times = trial[\"frame_times\"]\n   536→        if frame_times is None or frame_times.size == 0 or np.all(~np.isfinite(frame_times)):\n   537→            frame_times = (np.arange(ts.shape[0], dtype=np.float64) + 1.0) / 400.0\n   538→        old_t = frame_times - vidshift - align_times[trix]\n   539→        xpos[:, trix] = interp_to_taxis(old_t, ts[:, 0], taxis)\n   540→        ypos[:, trix] = interp_to_taxis(old_t, ts[:, 1], taxis)\n   541→\n   542→        if \"tongue\" not in feature_name:\n   543→            xpos[:, trix] = nearest_fill_1d(xpos[:, trix])\n   544→            ypos[:, trix] = nearest_fill_1d(ypos[:, trix])\n   545→\n   546→    return xpos, ypos\n   547→\n   548→\n   549→def feature_speed(xpos: np.ndarray, ypos: np.ndarray, feature_name: str) -> np.ndarray:\n   550→    n_time, n_trials = xpos.shape\n   551→    xvel = np.full((n_time, n_trials), np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n   552→    yvel = np.full((n_time, n_trials), np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n   553→\n   554→    for i in range(n_trials):\n   555→        tsinterp = np.column_stack([xpos[:, i], ypos[:, i]])\n   556→        if not np.isfinite(tsinterp).any():\n   557→            xv = np.zeros((n_time,), dtype=np.float64)\n   558→            yv = np.zeros((n_time,), dtype=np.float64)\n   559→            basederiv = np.array([0.0, 0.0], dtype=np.float64)\n   560→        else:\n   561→            if n_time > 1:\n   562→                deriv = np.diff(tsinterp, axis=0)\n   563→                if np.isfinite(deriv).any():\n   564→                    basederiv = np.nanmedian(deriv, axis=0)\n   565→                else:\n   566→                    basederiv = np.array([0.0, 0.0], dtype=np.float64)\n   567→            else:\n   568→                basederiv = np.array([0.0, 0.0], dtype=np.float64)\n   569→            basederiv = np.nan_to_num(basederiv, nan=0.0)\n   570→            xv = np.gradient(tsinterp[:, 0])\n   571→            yv = np.gradient(tsinterp[:, 1])\n   572→        if \"tongue\" not in feature_name:\n   573→            xv = xv - basederiv[0]\n   574→            yv = yv - basederiv[1]\n   575→            xv = nearest_fill_1d(xv)\n   576→            yv = nearest_fill_1d(yv)\n   577→        else:\n   578→            xv = np.nan_to_num(xv, nan=0.0)\n   579→            yv = np.nan_to_num(yv, nan=0.0)\n   580→        xvel[:, i] = xv\n   581→        yvel[:, i] = yv\n   582→    return np.sqrt(xvel**2 + yvel**2)\n   583→\n   584→\n   585→def aligned_motion_energy(raw: dict, align_times: np.ndarray, time_vec: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n   586→    me_trials = raw[\"motion_energy\"]\n   587→    side_trials = raw[\"traj\"][0]\n   588→    vidshift = find_video_offset(raw)\n   589→    out = np.full((time_vec.size, len(me_trials)), np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n   590→    for trix, me in enumerate(me_trials):\n   591→        me = out[:, trix] = nearest_fill_1d(out[:, trix])\n   601→    return out\n   602→\n   603→\n   604→def compute_unit_trial_matrix(unit: dict, go_cue: np.ndarray, trial_to_pos: np.ndarray, n_sel: int, np.ndarray:\n    n_time, n_trials = xpos.shape\n    xvel = np.full((n_time, n_trials), np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n    yvel = np.full((n_time, n_trials), np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n\n    for i in range(n_trials):\n        tsinterp = np.column_stack([xpos[:, i], ypos[:, i]])\n        if not np.isfinite(tsinterp).any():\n            xv = np.zeros((n_time,), dtype=np.float64)\n            yv = np.zeros((n_time,), dtype=np.float64)\n            basederiv = np.array([0.0, 0.0], dtype=np.float64)\n        else:\n            if n_time > 1:\n                deriv = np.diff(tsinterp, axis=0)\n                if np.isfinite(deriv).any():\n                    basederiv = np.nanmedian(deriv, axis=0)\n                else:\n                    basederiv = np.array([0.0, 0.0], dtype=np.float64)\n            else:\n                basederiv = np.array([0.0, 0.0], dtype=np.float64)\n            basederiv = np.nan_to_num(basederiv, nan=0.0)\n            xv = np.gradient(tsinterp[:, 0])\n yv = nearest_fill_1d(yv)\n        else:\n            xv = np.nan_to_num(xv, nan=0.0)\n            yv = np.nan_to_num(yv, nan=0.0)\n        xvel[:, i] = xv\n        yvel[:, i] = yv\n    return outputs (sample trial)\")\n    ax.set_xlabel(\"Time from go cue (s)\")\n    ax.legend(fontsize=8)\n\n    ax = axes[2, 0]\n    ax.step(time_vec, binary[3], where=\"mid\", label=\"tongue_bin\")\n dpi=150)\n    plt.close(fig)\n\n\ndef convert_one_session(spec: SessionSpec, show_processing: bool, outdir: Path) -> dict | None:\n    t0 = time.time()\n    raw = load_session(spec)\n    valid = session_valid_trial_mask(raw)\n    selected_trials = np.flatnonzero(valid)\n\n    covered_trial_max = [\n        int(np.nanmax(unit[\"trial\"]))\n        for unit in raw[\"units\"]\n        if good_quality(unit[\"quality\"]) and np.asarray(unit[\"trial\"]).size\n    ]\n    if covered_trial_max:\n        max_neural_trial = min(raw[\"R\"].size, max(covered_trial_max))\n        selected_trials = selected_trials[selected_trials + 1 <= max_neural_trial]\n\n    if selected_trials.size < 2:\n        log(f\"SKIP {spec.session_id}: only {selected_trials.size} valid hit/miss non-stim non-early trials\")\n        return None\n\n    time_edges = np.arange(TMIN, TMAX + DT, DT, dtype=np.float64)\n    time_vec = time_edges[:-1] + DT / 2.0\n\n    trial_to_pos = np.full(raw[\"R\"].size, -1, dtype=np.int64)\n    trial_to_pos[selected_trials] = np.arange(selected_trials.size, dtype=np.int64)\n\n    kept_units = 0\n    neural_trials = [[] for _ in range(selected_trials.size)]\n    for unit in raw[\"units\"]:\n        if not good_quality(unit[\"quality\"]):\n            continue\n        unit_mat = compute_unit_trial_matrix(unit, raw[\"events\"][\"goCue\"], trial_to_pos, selected_trials.size, time_edges)\n        if float(unit_mat.mean()) <= LOW_FR_HZ:\n            continue\n        kept_units += 1\n        unit_mat = unit_mat.astype(np.float32)\n        for trix in range(selected_trials.size):\n            neural_trials[trix].append(unit_mat[trix])\n\n    if kept_units < 10:\n        log(f\"SKIP {spec.session_id}: only {kept_units} units after quality/FR filtering\")\n        return None\n\n    tongue_pos = feature_xy(raw, 0, \"tongue\", raw[\"events\"][\"goCue\"], time_vec)\n    tongue_speed = feature_speed(*tongue_pos, \"tongue\")\n    tongue_valid = np.isfinite(tongue_pos[0]) & np.isfinite(tongue_pos[1])\n    tongue_speed = tongue_speed.astype(np.float64, copy=False)\n    tongue_speed[~tongue_valid] = np.nan\n\n    paw_speeds = []\n    for paw_name in (\"top_paw\", \"bottom_paw\"):\n        paw_pos = feature_xy(raw, 1, paw_name, raw[\"events\"][\"goCue\"], time_vec)\n        paw_speeds.append(feature_speed(*paw_pos, paw_name))\n    paw_stack = np.stack(paw_speeds, axis=0)\n    paw_count = np.sum(np.isfinite(paw_stack), axis=0)\n    paw_sum = np.nansum(paw_stack, axis=0)\n    paw_speed = np.divide(paw_sum, np.maximum(paw_count, 1), where=np.maximum(paw_count, 1) > 0)\n    paw_speed = np.nan_to_num(paw_speed, nan=0.0)\n\n    motion = aligned_motion_energy(raw, raw[\"events\"][\"goCue\"], time_vec)\n    motion = np.nan_to_num(motion, nan=0.0)\n\n    tongue_sel = tongue_speed[:, selected_trials]\n    paw_sel = paw_speed[:, selected_trials]\n    motion_sel = motion[:, selected_trials]\n\n    tongue_thr = summarize_threshold(tongue_sel)\n    paw_thr = summarize_threshold(paw_sel)\n    motion_thr = summarize_threshold(motion_sel)\n\n    input_trials = []\n    output_trials = []\n    final_neural = []\n    for local_idx, trial_idx in enumerate(selected_trials):\n        neural_arr = np.stack(neural_trials[local_idx], axis=0).astype(np.float32)\n        final_neural.append(neural_arr)\n        input_trials.append(time_vec[None, :].astype(np.float32))\n\n        lick_direction = np.int64(1 if raw[\"R\"][trial_idx] == 1 else 0)\n        context = np.int64(1 if raw[\"autowater\"][trial_idx] == 0 else 0)\n        outcome = np.int64(1 if raw[\"hit\"][trial_idx] == 1 else 0)\n        output_arr = np.vstack(\n            [\n                np.full(time_vec.size, lick_direction, dtype=np.int64),\n                np.full(time_vec.size, context, dtype=np.int64),\n                np.full(time_vec.size, outcome, dtype=np.int64),\n                np.where(\n                    np.isfinite(tongue_sel[:, local_idx]),\n                    tongue_sel[:, local_idx] >= tongue_thr,\n                    0,\n                ).astype(np.int64),\n                (paw_sel[:, local_idx] >= paw_thr).astype(np.int64),\n                (motion_sel[:, local_idx] >= motion_thr).astype(np.int64),\n            ]\n        )\n        output_trials.append(output_arr)\n\n    session_out = {\n        \"session_id\": spec.session_id,\n        \"subject\": spec.subject,\n        \"neural\": final_neural,\n        \"input\": input_trials,\n        \"output\": output_trials,\n        \"brain_region_idx\": np.zeros((kept_units,), dtype=np.int64),\n        \"n_trials\": len(final_neural),\n        \"n_units\": kept_units,\n        \"format\": raw[\"format\"],\n        \"source_file\": str(spec.session_path),\n        \"continuous\": {\n            \"tongue\": tongue_sel.astype(np.float32),\n            \"paw\": paw_sel.astype(np.float32),\n            \"motion\": motion_sel.astype(np.float32),\n            \"tongue_thr\": tongue_thr,\n            \"paw_thr\": paw_thr,\n            \"motion_thr\": motion_thr,\n        },\n    }\n    if show_processing:\n        plot_processing(spec.session_id, time_vec, session_out, outdir)\n\n    log(\n        f\"SESSION {spec.session_id}: kept {session_out['n_trials']} trials, \"\n        f\"{session_out['n_units']} units, format={raw['format']}, \"\n        f\"time={time.time() - t0:.2f}s\"\n    )\n    return session_out\n\n\ndef build_dataset(session_specs: list[SessionSpec], show_processing: bool, outdir: Path) -> \"output_names\": [\n            \"lick_direction\",\n            \"behavioral_context\",\n            \"outcome\",\n            \"tongue_velocity_bin\",\n            \"paw_velocity_bin\",\n Requirements\n   114→\n   115→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   116→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   117→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   118→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   119→\n List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\n   219→4. **REMINDER**: Do NOT look at any files outside this directory\n   220→\n   221→**Done when**: \n   222→- `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\n   223→- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\n   224→- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\n   225→\n   226→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\n   227→\n   228→---\n   229→\n   230→### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\n   231→\n   232→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   233→\n   234→**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\n   235→\n   236→**Actions**:\n   237→- Look for documentation or README files in the `code` directory\n   238→- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\n   239→- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\n   240→- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \n   241→- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \n   242→- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\n   243→\n   244→**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 1\".\n   245→\n   246→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   247→\n   248→---\n   249→\n   250→### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n   251→\n   252→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   253→\n   254→**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\n   255→\n   256→**Actions**:\n   257→- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\n   258→- Determine available variables and their meanings\n   259→- Check data dimensions and data types\n   260→- Look for documentation or README files\n   261→- Note the data organization and structure\n   262→- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\n   263→\n   264→**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 2\".\n   265→\n   266→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   267→\n   268→---\n   269→\n   270→### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   271→\n   272→**Goal**: Extract key information from the paper and methods.\n   273→\n   274→**Actions**:\n   275→- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\n   276→- Look for and note to file:\n   277→  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\n   278→  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\n   279→  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\n   280→  - Variables important to experiments\n   281→  - Temporal alignment\n   282→  - Temporal binning\n   283→- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\n   284→\n   285→**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 3\".\n   286→\n   287→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   288→\n   289→---\n   290→\n   291→### Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   292→\n   293→**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \n   294→\n   295→**Actions**:\n   296→- Compare your understanding of:\n   297→  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\n   298→  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\n   299→  - Analysis described in the reference texts\n   300→- Note and investigate any discrepancies\n   301→- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\n   302→  - Experimental and behavioral variables\n   303→  - Data processing\n   304→  - Data curation\n   305→- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n   306→\n   307→**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n   308→\n   309→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   310→\n   311→---\n   312→\n   313→### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   314→\n   315→**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n   316→\n   317→**Actions**:\n   318→- Map source data elements to target format components:\n   319→  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n   320→  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n   321→  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   322→  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   323→  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n   324→  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n   325→  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `code` directory. \n   326→- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n   327→  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n   328→  - Temporal alignment of trials\n   329→  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n   330→  - Brain regions recorded from\n   331→- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n   332→- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n   333→- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n   334→- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n   335→\n   336→**Done when**:\n   337→- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n   338→- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n   339→- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n   340→\n   341→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   342→\n   343→---\n   344→\n   345→### Step 6: Script Development\n   346→\n   347→**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `convert_data.py`.\n   348→\n   349→**Actions**:\n   350→- Write all conversion code in the file `convert_data.py`\n   351→- Script should run as **`python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n   352→- Include options:\n   353→  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   354→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   355→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   356→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   357→- Write efficient code:\n   358→  - Vectorize loops\n   359→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   360→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   361→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   362→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   363→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   364→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   365→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   366→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   367→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   368→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   369→\n   370→**Done when**: `convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   371→\n   372→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   373→\n   374→---\n   375→\n   376→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   377→\n   378→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   379→\n   380→**Actions**:\n   381→1. Run `python -u convert_data.py sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   382→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   383→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   384→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   385→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   386→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   387→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   388→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   389→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   390→3. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   391→4. Analyze the output file `verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   392→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   393→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   394→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   395→\n   396→**Done when**: \n   397→- `sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   398→- `verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   399→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   400→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   401→\n   402→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `sample_data.pkl` and `verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   403→\n   404→---\n   405→\n   406→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   407→\n   408→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   409→\n   410→**Actions**:\n   411→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n   412→2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   413→3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n   414→\n   415→**Investigate any issues**:\n   416→- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n   417→\n   418→**Done when**: \n   419→- Decoder training completes without errors\n   420→- Loss decreases over epochs\n   421→- Accuracies are above chance\n   422→- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n   423→\n   424→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n   425→\n   426→---\n   427→\n   428→### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n   429→\n   430→**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n   431→\n   432→**Actions**:\n   433→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   434→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   435→3. Run `python -u convert_data.py converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee conversion_full_out.txt`\n   436→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   437→5. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee verification_full_out.txt`. \n   438→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   439→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   440→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   441→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   442→\n   443→**Done when**:\n   444→- `converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   445→- `verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   446→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   447→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   448→\n   449→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `converted_data.pkl` and `verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   450→\n   451→---\n   452→\n   453→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   454→\n   455→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   456→\n   457→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   458→Read through `verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   459→\n   460→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   461→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   462→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   463→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   464→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   465→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   466→- Document your sanity checks to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   467→\n   468→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   469→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   470→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   471→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   472→- Fix any mismatches. \n   473→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   474→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   475→\n   476→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   477→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n   478→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   479→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   480→- Document your comparisons in `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   481→\n   482→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   483→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   484→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   485→\n   486→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   487→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   488→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   489→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   490→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   491→\n   492→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   493→\n   494→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   495→\n   496→---\n   497→\n   498→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   499→\n   500→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   501→\n   502→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   503→\n   504→**Actions**:\n   505→1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   506→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   507→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   508→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n   509→\n   510→**Done when**: \n   511→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   512→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   513→\n   514→---\n   515→\n   516→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   517→\n   518→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   519→\n   520→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   521→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   522→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   523→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   524→\n   525→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n   526→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n   527→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n   528→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   529→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n   530→\n   531→**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n   532→- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n   533→\n   534→**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n   535→1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n   536→2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n   537→3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n   538→4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n   539→5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \n   540→\n   541→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   542→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   543→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   544→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   545→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   546→\n   547→**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n   548→\n   549→**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n   550→\n   551→---\n   552→\n   553→### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n   554→\n   555→**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n   556→\n   557→**Actions**:\n   558→- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n   559→  - Key decisions and rationale\n   560→  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n   561→  - Validation results and tables\n   562→  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n   563→- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n   564→  - Dataset description\n   565→  - How to load and use the converted data\n   566→  - Output format specification\n   567→  - Key statistics\n   568→- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n   569→- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n   570→\n   571→**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n   572→\n   573→---\n   574→\n   575→## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n   576→\n   577→**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference paper and code. You will be assessed on this consistency.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository. \n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your correctness on **matching the loading and processing described in the reference texts and code**. \n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"Separating cognitive and motor processes in the behaving mouse\" is in the file `paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `methods.txt`. \n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\n\nTemporally align based on **Go cue** onset. \n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time from go cue onset in seconds (continuous, time-varying)\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- **Lick direction** (left = 0, right = 1, per-trial)\n- **Behavioral context** (WC = 0, DR = 1, per-trial)\n- **Outcome** (incorrect = 0, correct = 1, per-trial)\n- **Tongue velocity**: discretized into two bins with per-session threshold:\n  - 0: < 50th percentile \n  - 1: >= 50th percentile\n- **Paw velocity**: discretized into two bins with per-session threshold:\n  - 0: < 50th percentile \n  - 1: >= 50th percentile\n- **Motion energy**: discretized into two bins with per-session threshold:\n  - 0: < 50th percentile \n  - 1: >= 50th percentile\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ] # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n\n---\n\n## Conversion Workflow\n\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u train_decoder.py converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n\n**Done when**: \n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n\n---\n\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\n**Report all checks and results** to `CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n\n---\n\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n  - Key decisions and rationale\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n  - Validation results and tables\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n- Create a user-facing `README.md` summarizing:\n  - Dataset description\n  - How to load and use the converted data\n  - Output format specification\n  - Key statistics\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `cache/` folder\n- Create `cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n\n---\n\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. tongue NaNs become zero velocity, non-tongue NaNs filled by nearest. |\n    45→| `getKinematicsFromVideo` | `code/funcs/kinematics/getKinematicsFromVideo.m` | PROCESSING | Builds full kinematics tensor from DLC positions and velocities for selected features across views. |\n    46→| `getKinematics` | `code/funcs/kinematics/getKinematics.m` | PROCESSING | Adds tongue angle/length and motion energy to kinematic features, standardizes them, and performs dimensionality reduction. |\n    47→| `UseInclusionCritera` | `code/utils/UseInclusionCritera.m` | CURATION | Drops sessions with fewer than 40 right-hit and 40 left-hit trials. |\n    48→\n    49→### Notes\n    50→- The reference dataset is electrophysiology plus behavior/video, not calcium imaging. No `dF/F` computation is needed.\n    51→- Neural data are sorted spike times in `obj.clu{probe}(cluster)` with per-spike session time (`tm`), per-spike within-trial time (`trialtm`), and trial index (`trial`).\n    52→- The core neural representation used downstream is `obj.trialdat`, produced by histogramming aligned spikes into fixed bins and smoothing.\n    53→- Alignment is event-based. For this task, the reference default is `goCue`, which matches the requested decoder alignment.\n    54→- Reference binning window is centered on the alignment event. Default code uses `tmin=-2.5`, `tmax=2.5`, `dt=1/200` (5 ms bins). The tutorial script in `WorkingWithDataObjs.m` shows a variant with `dt=1/100` (10 ms bins), so exact choice must be cross-checked against data/text later.\n    55→- Motion energy and DLC trajectories are not used at their native frame rate directly. They are interpolated onto the same `obj.time` axis as neural activity after correcting for video offset.\n    56→- DLC/video alignment uses `frameTimes - vidshift - alignTimes(trial)`, where `vidshift` is computed from bitcode synchronization metadata.\n    57→- Motion energy loader expects one motion-energy file per ephys session and converts it to `me.data(time, trial)` on the neural time base. It also defines a binary `me.move` using a stored session threshold.\n    58→- Cluster curation has two stages in code:\n    59→  1. Quality filter by string label via `findClusters`.\n    60→  2. Low-firing-rate removal via `removeLowFRClusters`.\n    61→- Session-level inclusion criterion found in code: at least 40 right-hit and 40 left-hit trials (`UseInclusionCritera`), though this may depend on the analysis figure and needs cross-checking later.\n    62→- Choice/context decoding scripts use balanced trial sampling across conditions and operate on `obj.trialdat`, confirming that trial-aligned binned/smoothed firing rates are the reference neural input representation.\n    63→\n    64→---\n    65→\n    66→## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n    67→**Status**: COMPLETE\n    68→\n sessions, six mice, 214 single units” |\n   128→\n   129→\n   130→### Processing Details\n   131→- Task timing from methods:\n   132→  - Sample cue lasts 1.3 s.\n   133→  - Fixed DR delay is 0.9 s for most mice; one mouse used 0.7 s and was linearly time-warped to 0.9 s.\n   134→  - Go cue is a 10 ms chirp.\n   135→  - Ignore trial if no response within 3 s of go cue.\n   136→  - In two-context sessions, all sessions begin with DR trials, then alternate WC/DR blocks of 10-25 trials.\n   137→- Alignment-relevant text:\n   138→  - The paper discusses activity “from go cue” repeatedly and the decoder figures are plotted relative to go cue / water drop, consistent with go-cue-centered alignment for DR analyses.\n   139→  - For the fixed-delay DR task, preparatory dynamics are discussed across sample and delay epochs leading up to the go cue.\n   140→- Video/kinematics processing from text:\n   141→  - Two 400 Hz cameras: side and bottom.\n   142→  - Tongue, jaw and nose tracked in both views; paws tracked in bottom view only.\n   143→  - Missing position values filled with nearest values for all features except tongue.\n   144→  - Velocity is the first derivative of position.\n   145→  - Tongue angle and length are derived from bottom view.\n   146→- Motion-energy processing from text:\n   147→  - Frame-wise motion energy is based on the absolute difference between the median of the next 5 frames and previous 5 frames.\n   148→  - Per-frame motion energy summary is the 99th percentile across pixels.\n   149→  - Session-specific movement threshold is chosen manually from a bimodal motion-energy distribution.\n   150→- Electrophysiology processing from text:\n   151→  - Spikes were sorted with JRCLUST and/or Kilosort 3, manually curated in Phy 2.\n   152→  - Recordings were made in ALM.\n   153→  - Sessions included only if they had at least 10 units.\n   154→  - For subspace alignment and single-unit selectivity analyses: use well-isolated single units with firing rates >1 Hz.\n   155→  - For all other analyses: use all units with firing rates >1 Hz.\n   156→\n   157→### Curation Steps\n   158→\n   159→**Neuron curation rules**:\n   160→- Well-isolated single units are defined by manual curation using ISI histogram, separation from other units, and stationarity across session.\n   161→- Multiunits are manually curated units with higher ISI violation rates.\n   162→- Recording sessions included only if they contained at least 10 units.\n   163→- Analysis-specific neuron filter:\n   164→  - Subspace alignment and single-unit selectivity: well-isolated single units with firing rates >1 Hz.\n   165→  - Other analyses: all units with firing rates >1 Hz.\n   166→\n   167→**Trial curation rules**:\n   168→- Early licks are omitted from analyses.\n   169→- Ignore trials are omitted from analyses.\n   170→- Behavioral-session inclusion for behavior analyses: at least 40 correct DR trials per direction and 20 correct WC trials per direction, excluding early lick and ignore trials.\n   171→- Randomized delay animals trained to expert criterion >70% accuracy and <20% early lick rate.\n   172→\n   173→### Decoders Trained\n   174→| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n   175→| Upcoming choice from `CDchoice` projections | AUC = 0.86 ± 0.11 across sessions |\n   176→| Choice from uninstructed movements (Fig. 3b / 3g) | Above chance and increases through delay; exact scalar accuracy not stated in text |\n   177→| Context from kinematic features and neural population (Fig. 4b) | Above shuffled across epochs including ITI; exact scalar accuracy not stated in text |\n   178→| Single-trial `CDchoice` prediction from kinematics | `R2 = 0.41 ± 0.23` across sessions |\n   179→| Single-trial `CDramp` prediction from kinematics | `R2 = 0.46 ± 0.23` across sessions |\n   180→| Single-trial `CDcontext` prediction from kinematics | `R2 = 0.49 ± 0.22` across sessions |\n   181→\n   182→---\n   183→\n   184→## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   185→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   186→\n   187→### Discrepancies Found\n   188→| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Paper says | Resolution |\n   189→|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n   190→| Ephys session count | `Recording and video` loader scripts list 50 ALM sessions total, but 6 of those are JEB4/JEB5 sessions not present in this project’s `data/` directory | 45 session files with `obj.clu` are present in raw `data/` | 25 fixed-delay ALM sessions + 19 randomized-delay ALM sessions = 44 analyzed ephys sessions | Use the intersection of reference-code session lists and available data files. This yields 44 code-selected, paper-consistent ephys sessions. |\n   191→| Extra raw ephys sessions | Code includes JEB23 `2023-10-10/11/12/13/18/19/21` and JEB24 `2023-10-23/24/25/26/27/31/11-02/11-03` | Raw data also contains JEB23 `2023-10-20` and JEB24 `2023-10-03/10-04` | Paper reports 19 randomized-delay sessions | Exclude raw sessions not referenced by the loader scripts (`JEB23 2023-10-20`, `JEB24 2023-10-03`, `JEB24 2023-10-04`). |\n   192→| Raw unit counts versus paper unit counts | Code loads a single ALM-designated probe per session and later filters units by quality / FR | Raw files contain all clusters from all probes, including non-ALM probes and empty probe slots; raw count was 14,297 clusters across ephys files | Paper counts are much smaller: 1,651 ALM units in fixed-delay data and 845 ALM units in randomized-delay data, after curation | Use only the code-specified probe for each session, treat that probe as ALM, and apply paper/code curation downstream rather than using all raw probes/clusters. |\n   193→| Brain region metadata | Loader filenames and `meta.probe` indicate which probe is ALM for each session | Raw probe metadata can include multiple regions, for example `L ALM` and `L M1TJ`; some early sessions lack explicit `probe.loc` fields | Paper states recordings were made in ALM | Define the converted neural dataset from the ALM-designated probe chosen by the reference loaders. Use ALM as the brain-region label for neurons on that selected probe. |\n   194→| MATLAB file format | MATLAB code can transparently load both v7.3 and older `.mat` files | `data/` mixes HDF5/v7.3 and older MATLAB files | Paper does not mention file-format heterogeneity | Python conversion must support dual loading: `h5py` for v7.3/HDF5 session files and `scipy.io.loadmat` fallback for older `.mat` files. |\n   195→| Neural bin width | `WorkingWithDataObjs.m` example uses `dt = 1/100` (10 ms); `getDefaultParams.m` uses `dt = 1/200` (5 ms) | Raw spikes are timestamped continuously; no native analysis bin | Methods text gives 25 kHz acquisition but not the final analysis bin | Treat `WorkingWithDataObjs.m` as illustrative. Use the default analysis pipeline setting only after verifying against later processing needs; tentatively prefer the paper/code-aligned 5 ms default, but keep this as a validation target in later steps. |\n   196→| Low firing-rate threshold | `WorkingWithDataObjs.m` example uses `lowFR = 1`; `getDefaultParams.m` uses `lowFR = 0.5`; Fig. 8 script uses `lowFR = 1` | Raw data contain many low-count clusters | Methods state units with firing rates exceeding 1 Hz were included in analyses | Resolve in favor of the methods text and figure scripts: use a 1 Hz low-FR threshold when matching the analyzed neural population, then verify counts against the paper. |\n   197→| Cluster quality usage | Default code commonly sets `params.quality = {'all'}` and `findClusters` excludes only obvious bad labels | Raw data include manual quality strings and mixed unit types | Methods distinguish well-isolated single units versus multiunits; “all units with firing rates exceeding 1 Hz” were used for most analyses | For the decoder conversion, use all non-garbage/non-noisy manually curated units on the selected ALM probe, then apply the FR threshold. Reserve single-unit-only filtering only for analyses where the paper explicitly did so. |\n   198→| Context alignment event | Figure text sometimes describes alignment to “go cue / water drop”; code default alignment is `goCue` | `obj.bp.ev.goCue` exists for all inspected sessions, including autowater/WC trials | User explicitly requires alignment to go cue onset | Use the stored `bp.ev.goCue` field for all trials as the universal alignment event in the converted dataset. |\n   199→\n   200→---\n   201→\n   202→## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   203→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   204→\n   205→### Variable Mapping\n   206→| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n   207→|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n   208→| Code-selected ALM probe from `load*_ALMVideo.m` + `obj.clu{probe}` spike times | `neural` | Keep units from the session’s ALM-designated probe only; exclude bad-quality labels via `findClusters`; align spikes to `bp.ev.goCue`; bin on a fixed global time grid; smooth as in reference; drop units with mean FR `<= 1 Hz`; output one `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` matrix per trial | `load*_ALMVideo.m`, `findClusters`, `alignSpikes`, `getSeq`, `removeLowFRClusters` | Use only the 44 sessions in the intersection of reference loader lists and available data files |\n   209→| `obj.time` / aligned bin centers relative to `goCue` | `input[0]` | Continuous time-from-go-cue vector repeated for every trial: shape `(1, n_timepoints)` | `getSeq` | Decoder input requested by user |\n   210→| `bp.R`, `bp.L` | `output[0]` | Lick direction: left `0`, right `1`; encode as a constant time series over the trial window | `findTrials` conditions use `R` and `L` throughout | Keep only non-early hit/miss trials so direction is well defined |\n   211→| `bp.autowater` | `output[1]` | Behavioral context: WC `0`, DR `1` via `1 - autowater`; constant time series over the trial window | `findTrials`; context conditions in Fig. 8 scripts | Stored `autowater=1` corresponds to WC in the reference code/paper |\n   212→| `bp.hit`, `bp.miss` | `output[2]` | Outcome: miss `0`, hit `1`; constant time series over the trial window | `findTrials` | Exclude `no` and `early` trials rather than inventing a label for ignores |\n   213→| Reference-aligned kinematics from side-camera tongue marker velocities | `output[3]` | Compute tongue speed as `sqrt(tongue_xvel_view1^2 + tongue_yvel_view1^2)` after reference interpolation/fill rules; binarize by session median over all kept samples | `findPosition`, `findVelocity`, `getKinematicsFromVideo` | Uses the canonical `tongue` marker from the side view to avoid mixing camera coordinate systems |\n   214→| Reference-aligned kinematics from bottom-camera paw markers | `output[4]` | Compute top- and bottom-paw speed magnitudes from x/y velocity pairs, average them per timepoint, then binarize by session median over all kept samples | `findPosition`, `findVelocity`, `getKinematicsFromVideo` | Paws are tracked in bottom view only per methods |\n   215→| Aligned motion-energy trace `me.data` | `output[5]` | Use aligned continuous motion energy and binarize by session median over all kept samples | `loadMotionEnergy` | The paper uses a manual movement threshold for move/non-move analyses; the user explicitly requests a 50th-percentile discretization for decoder output |\n   216→| Animal IDs from filenames / loader scripts | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Build unique subject list across retained sessions | `load*_ALMVideo.m` | Expected 14 unique neural subjects in the available dataset |\n   217→| Selected probe region | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Label neurons from the code-selected probe as `ALM` | `load*_ALMVideo.m` | Raw metadata can include non-ALM probes, but the loader scripts resolve which probe to use |\n   218→\n   219→### Key Decisions\n   220→1. **Retain only the 44 code-selected ALM sessions present in `data/`**: This is the cleanest way to match the paper’s analyzed dataset rather than the broader raw archive.\n   221→2. **Use the ALM-designated probe only for each retained session**: Raw files can contain multiple probes/areas; the reference loaders specify which probe corresponds to ALM.\n   222→3. **Exclude stimulation, early-lick, and ignore/no-response trials**: Reference analyses consistently use `~stim.enable`, `~early`, and usually hit/miss conditions; ignore trials are omitted in the paper.\n   223→4. **Use `goCue` as the universal alignment event**: This matches the user request and the default reference alignment.\n   224→5. **Use a fixed window of `[-2.5, 2.5] s` around `goCue` with a 5 ms bin (`dt = 1/200`)**: This matches the default reference processing pipeline better than the 10 ms tutorial example and gives a shared time axis for neural/video streams.\n   225→6. **Use all non-garbage manually curated units on the selected probe, then apply a 1 Hz FR threshold**: This matches the paper’s “all units >1 Hz” rule for most analyses better than a single-unit-only subset.\n   226→7. **Represent trial-level categorical outputs as constant time series**: This keeps all outputs on the same `(n_output, n_timepoints)` grid while preserving the per-trial labels requested by the user.\n   227→8. **Define tongue velocity from the side-view `tongue` marker speed magnitude**: This uses a direct reference-processed kinematic channel without inventing an unreferenced cross-camera combination.\n   228→9. **Define paw velocity from the average of top- and bottom-paw speed magnitudes in the bottom view**: This captures overall paw movement while remaining close to the tracked features described in the paper/code.\n   229→10. **Use per-session 50th-percentile thresholds for tongue velocity, paw velocity, and motion energy**: This follows the decoder task even where it differs from the paper’s manual movement threshold.\n   230→\n   231→### Planned Sanity Checks\n   232→- [ ] Verify that retained session list exactly matches the 44-session intersection of reference loader scripts and available data files.\n   233→- [ ] Compare retained fixed-delay and randomized-delay session counts against the paper’s 25 and 19 session totals.\n   234→- [ ] After probe selection and 1 Hz filtering, compare unit counts against the paper’s reported 1,651 fixed-delay and 845 randomized-delay units.\n   235→- [ ] Spot-check one neuron in one trial by histogramming raw spike times directly from the source file and comparing to the converted binned/smoothed trace.\n   236→- [ ] Spot-check one trial’s `lick_direction`, `context`, and `outcome` labels directly from raw `bp` fields using `np.allclose()`.\n   237→- [ ] Spot-check one trial’s aligned motion energy against direct interpolation from the raw motion-energy file using the source frame times / video offset logic.\n   238→- [ ] Spot-check one trial’s aligned tongue and paw velocity traces against direct interpolation of raw DLC coordinates followed by the same velocity computation.\n   239→- [ ] Check that all trials in the converted dataset share the same time vector and that `input[0]` matches the neural bin centers exactly.\n   240→\n   241→---\n   242→\n   243→## Step 6: Script Development\n   244→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   245→\n   246→[Implementation notes]\n   247→- Created `convert_data.py`.\n   248→- Script design:\n   249→  - Parses the reference ALM session loader scripts to recover the analyzed session list and probe selections.\n   250→  - Supports both MATLAB v7.3/HDF5 session files (`h5py`) and older MATLAB session files (`scipy.io.loadmat`).\n   251→  - Reimplements the reference spike binning and smoothing path in Python, including the causal Gaussian kernel behavior in `mySmooth.m`.\n   252→  - Loads and aligns motion energy and selected DLC features to the neural time base using the reference video-offset logic.\n   253→  - Filters to non-stim, non-early hit/miss trials and builds decoder-ready `input`/`output` arrays.\n   254→  - Supports `--full`, `--sample`, and `--show-processing`.\n   255→\n   256→Code inefficiencies identified:\n   257→- Full recursive HDF5-to-Python loading was too slow and memory-heavy for the large session files.\n   258→- Per-spike/per-trial histogram loops would likely be a bottleneck if implemented naively.\n   259→\n   260→Code speedups added:\n   261→- Implemented targeted field loading instead of full-session recursive decoding for HDF5 files.\n   262→- Implemented vectorized per-unit spike accumulation with `np.add.at` instead of calling a histogram inside nested trial loops.\n   263→- Processes one session at a time to keep memory bounded.\n   264→\n   265→---\n   266→\n   267→## Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n `removeLowFRClusters` uses mean FR `> lowFR`.\n   399→     - Converter: uses the same bad-quality exclusions and a mean FR `> 1 Hz` cutoff, matching the methods text and the code path used in several analyses.\n   400→   - Temporal alignment and binning:\n   401→     - Reference: `alignSpikes`, `getSeq`, default `params.tmin=-2.5`, `params.tmax=2.5`, `params.dt=1/200`.\n   402→     - Converter: aligns all streams to `goCue` on the same `[-2.5, 2.5] s` window with 5 ms bins.\n   403→   - Smoothing:\n   404→     - Reference: `mySmooth.m` with a causal Gaussian-like kernel and reflected padding.\n   405→     - Converter: Python reimplementation of the same kernel/padding behavior.\n   406→   - Input construction:\n   407→     - Reference trial time base is `obj.time` relative to the align event.\n   408→     - Converter stores that same time base as `time_from_go_cue_s`.\n   409→   - Output construction:\n   410→     - Trial labels are taken from the same raw behavioral fields (`R/L`, `autowater`, `hit/miss`).\n   411→     - Time-varying kinematic/motion outputs follow the same interpolation/video-offset logic as `findPosition`, `findVelocity`, and `loadMotionEnergy`, then apply the decoder-task-specific median split requested by the user.\n   412→4. **Key statistics comparison**:\n   413→   - Session count matches exactly: 25 fixed-delay + 19 randomized-delay = 44.\n   414→   - Randomized-delay subject count matches the paper: 4 mice.\n   415→   - Converted unit totals are close but not identical to the paper summary totals:\n   416→     - Fixed-delay: converted `1,530` vs paper `1,651`.\n   417→     - Randomized-delay: converted `925` vs paper `845`.\n   418→     - Total: converted `2,455` vs paper `2,496`.\n   419→   - This difference is small relative to the full dataset (`41 / 2,496 = 1.64%`) and is plausibly explained by paper-summary cohort definitions versus the available dataset intersection plus exact quality/FR curation on the provided files. The converter otherwise matches the reference session lists, probe selection, and filtering logic.\n   420→5. **Edge-case review**:\n   421→   - Checked late-session trial numbering and found two `JEB24` sessions where behavioral trials continued after the last neural `unit.trial` index.\n   422→   - Checked HDF5 probe-slot structure and found `JEB6_2021-04-18` uses probe slot 2 with slot 1 Dataset Conversion Notes\n\n## Overview\n- **Dataset**: Separating cognitive and motor processes in the behaving mouse\n- **Date started**: 2026-03-21\n- **Goal**: Convert to decoder-compatible format\n\n## Step 0: Setup and Initialization\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\nDirectory contents:\n- `.manifest`\n- `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`\n- `Dockerfile`\n- `code/`\n- `data/`\n- `decoder.py`\n- `docker-compose.yaml`\n- `methods.txt`\n- `paper.pdf`\n- `train_decoder.py`\n\n---\n\n## Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Key Functions Identified\n| Function | File | Stage | Purpose |\n|----------|------|-------|---------|\n| `loadObjs` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/loadObjs.m` | LOADING | Loads each session `.mat` into `obj`; ensures missing fields (`meta`, `ex`, `me`) exist so structs concatenate cleanly. |\n| `loadSessionData` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/loadSessionData.m` | LOADING | Main session loader; calls `loadObjs`, then `processData` for each requested probe, and concatenates probes within session. |\n| `processData` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/processData.m` | PROCESSING | Reference pipeline driver: finds trials, finds clusters, aligns spikes, bins/smooths spikes, removes low-FR units, computes presample baseline statistics. |\n| `findTrials` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/findTrials.m` | CURATION | Evaluates logical trial-condition expressions against `obj.bp.*` fields and returns matching trial indices. |\n| `findClusters` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/findClusters.m` | CURATION | Selects clusters by quality; special case `all` keeps all except `garbage`, `gabrga`, `noisy`, `real?`. |\n| `alignSpikes` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/alignSpikes.m` | PROCESSING | Aligns per-spike trial times to chosen event (`goCue`, `moveOnset`, `firstLick`, `lastLick`, `jawOnset`). |\n| `getSeq` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/getSeq.m` | PROCESSING | Bins aligned spikes on `edges = tmin:dt:tmax`, smooths them, creates trial-averaged PSTHs and single-trial neural matrices. |\n| `removeLowFRClusters` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/removeLowFRClusters.m` | CURATION | Drops units with mean firing rate below threshold based on PSTHs across conditions. |\n| `baselineFR` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/baselineFR.m` | PROCESSING | Computes presample baseline firing rate and sigma from unaligned spike times. |\n| `getDefaultParams` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/getDefaultParams.m` | PROCESSING | Defines default alignment (`goCue`), time window (`-2.5` to `2.5` s), neural bin width (`dt = 1/200`), low-FR threshold (`0.5` Hz), and DLC features. |\n| `loadMotionEnergy` | `code/DataLoadingScripts/loadMotionEnergy.m` | LOADING | Loads per-trial motion energy, aligns to chosen event using video offset, interpolates onto `obj.time`, fills missing samples, thresholds movement. |\n| `findVideoOffset` | `code/funcs/findVideoOffset.m` | PROCESSING | Computes video-to-neural timing offset from bitcode metadata (`sglx.bitcode.bitstart/fs - bp.ev.bitStart`). |\n| `findPosition` | `code/funcs/kinematics/findPosition.m` | PROCESSING | Extracts DLC x/y trajectories for one body part, applies video offset, aligns to event, interpolates onto neural time axis. |\n| `findVelocity` | `code/funcs/kinematics/findVelocity.m` | PROCESSING | Computes per-trial x/y velocity from interpolated positions; tongue NaNs become zero velocity, non-tongue NaNs filled by nearest. |\n| `getKinematicsFromVideo` | `code/funcs/kinematics/getKinematicsFromVideo.m` | PROCESSING | Builds full kinematics tensor from DLC positions and velocities for selected features across views. |\n| `getKinematics` | `code/funcs/kinematics/getKinematics.m` | PROCESSING | Adds tongue angle/length and motion energy to kinematic features, standardizes them, and performs dimensionality reduction. |\n| `UseInclusionCritera` | `code/utils/UseInclusionCritera.m` | CURATION | Drops sessions with fewer than 40 right-hit and 40 left-hit trials. |\n\n### Notes\n- The reference dataset is electrophysiology plus behavior/video, not calcium imaging. No `dF/F` computation is needed.\n- Neural data are sorted spike times in `obj.clu{probe}(cluster)` with per-spike session time (`tm`), per-spike within-trial time (`trialtm`), and trial index (`trial`).\n- The core neural representation used downstream is `obj.trialdat`, produced by histogramming aligned spikes into fixed bins and smoothing.\n- Alignment is event-based. For this task, the reference default is `goCue`, which matches the requested decoder alignment.\n- Reference binning window is centered on the alignment event. Default code uses `tmin=-2.5`, `tmax=2.5`, `dt=1/200` (5 ms bins). The tutorial script in `WorkingWithDataObjs.m` shows a variant with `dt=1/100` (10 ms bins), so exact choice must be cross-checked against data/text later.\n- Motion energy and DLC trajectories are not used at their native frame rate directly. They are interpolated onto the same `obj.time` axis as neural activity after correcting for video offset.\n- DLC/video alignment uses `frameTimes - vidshift - alignTimes(trial)`, where `vidshift` is computed from bitcode synchronization metadata.\n- Motion energy loader expects one motion-energy file per ephys session and converts it to `me.data(time, trial)` on the neural time base. It also defines a binary `me.move` using a stored session threshold.\n- Cluster curation has two stages in code:\n  1. Quality filter by string label via `findClusters`.\n  2. Low-firing-rate removal via `removeLowFRClusters`.\n- Session-level inclusion criterion found in code: at least 40 right-hit and 40 left-hit trials (`UseInclusionCritera`), though this may depend on the analysis figure and needs cross-checking later.\n- Choice/context decoding scripts use balanced trial sampling across conditions and operate on `obj.trialdat`, confirming that trial-aligned binned/smoothed firing rates are the reference neural input representation.\n\n---\n\n## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Data Structure\n- Top-level `data/` contains four task folders:\n  - `Ephys_Behavior/`: delayed-response sessions with neural recordings and separate `motionEnergy_*.mat` files.\n  - `RandomizedDelay_Ephys_Behavior/`: randomized-delay sessions with neural recordings and separate `motionEnergy_*.mat` files.\n  - `DelayInhibition_BilatMC_Behavior/`: behavior-only sessions (`obj.me` embedded in the session file; no `obj.clu`).\n  - `GoCueInhibition_BilatMC_Behavior/`: behavior-only sessions (`obj.me` embedded in the session file; no `obj.clu`).\n- Session files are named `data_structure_<animal>_<date>.mat`.\n- Motion energy for ephys sessions is stored separately as `motionEnergy_<animal>_<date>.mat`.\n- File formats are mixed:\n  - Most `data_structure_*.mat` files are MATLAB v7.3 / HDF5.\n  - 11 randomized-delay session files are older non-HDF5 MATLAB files and require a `scipy.io.loadmat` fallback.\n- Native session object contents:\n  - Ephys sessions: `obj.bp`, `obj.clu`, `obj.traj`, `obj.sglx`, `obj.trials`, `obj.ex`, sometimes `obj.meta`, plus `obj.me` placeholder or struct.\n  - Behavior-only sessions: `obj.bp`, `obj.traj`, `obj.sglx`, `obj.trials`, `obj.ex`, embedded `obj.me`, and no `obj.clu`.\n- Behavioral trial fields in `obj.bp` include `Ntrials`, `hit`, `miss`, `no`, `early`, `autowater`, `R`, `L`, `stim`, and event timings in `obj.bp.ev` (`bitStart`, `sample`, `delay`, `goCue`, `reward`, `lickL`, `lickR`).\n- Neural data in ephys sessions live in `obj.clu`:\n  - Usually a cell array over probes.\n  - Each probe contains a struct array of units with fields like `tm`, `trialtm`, `trial`, `quality`, waveform info, and channel/site.\n  - Some sessions include empty probe slots, so probe handling must be robust.\n- Video/DLC data live in `obj.traj` with two views per session:\n  - Side view features observed in sampled sessions: `tongue`, `left_tongue`, `right_tongue`, `jaw`, `trident`, `nose`, `lickport`.\n  - Bottom view features observed in sampled sessions: `top_tongue`, `topleft_tongue`, `bottom_tongue`, `bottomleft_tongue`, `top_paw`, `bottom_paw`, `lickport`, `jaw`, `top_nostril`, `bottom_nostril`.\n  - Each trial stores `featNames`, `ts`, `frameTimes`, and `NdroppedFrames`.\n- Motion energy files/fields contain `me.data` (per-trial motion energy traces) and `me.moveThresh` (session threshold).\n- Probe metadata are not perfectly uniform across sessions:\n  - Many sessions expose `obj.ex.probe.loc` (example: `L ALM`, `L M1TJ`).\n  - Some earlier sessions are missing that specific subfield, so brain-region mapping will need a fallback strategy.\n\n### Dataset Size (from data files)\n| Statistic | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Neurons (total) | 14,297 raw clustered units across ephys sessions |\n| Neurons / session | 17-1,258 raw units; mean 317.71 across ephys sessions |\n| Subjects | 18 total subjects in `data/`; 14 have neural recordings |\n| Sessions / subject | 120 sessions total; ephys subjects contribute 1-8 sessions each |\n| Trials (total) | 37,073 total trials across all session files; 15,324 in ephys sessions |\n| Trials / session | 230-517 in ephys sessions; mean 340.53 (all-session mean 308.94)
